{"type":"user","message":{"role":"user","content":[{"tool_use_id":"toolu_01LqqDsNbvArqjh7fukEcywp","type":"tool_result","content":"1\t.. _instruction-fetch:\n2\t\n3\tInstruction Fetch\n4\t=================\n5\t:file:`rtl/ibex_if_stage.sv.`\n6\t\n7\t.. figure:: images/if_stage.svg\n8\t   :name: if_stage\n9\t   :align: center\n10\t\n11\t   Instruction Fetch (IF) stage\n12\t\n13\tThe Instruction Fetch (IF) stage of the core is able to supply one instruction to the Instruction-Decode (ID) stage per cycle if the instruction cache or the instruction memory is able to serve one instruction per cycle.\n14\t\n15\tInstructions are fetched into a prefetch buffer (:file:`rtl/ibex_prefetch_buffer.sv`) for optimal performance and timing closure reasons.\n16\tThis buffer simply fetches instructions linearly until it is full.\n17\tThe instructions themselves are stored along with the Program Counter (PC) they came from in the fetch FIFO (:file:`rtl/ibex_fetch_fifo.sv`).\n18\tThe fetch FIFO has a feedthrough path so when empty a new instruction entering the FIFO is immediately made available on the FIFO output.\n19\tA localparam ``DEPTH`` gives a configurable depth which is set to 3 by default.\n20\t\n21\tThe top-level of the instruction fetch controls the prefetch buffer (in particular flushing it on branches/jumps/exception and beginning prefetching from the appropriate new PC) and supplies new instructions to the ID/EX stage along with their PC.\n22\tCompressed instructions are expanded by the IF stage so the decoder can always deal with uncompressed instructions (the ID stage still receives the compressed instruction for placing into ``mtval`` on an illegal instruction exception).\n23\t\n24\tIf Ibex has been configured with an instruction cache (parameter ICache == 1), then the prefetch buffer is replaced by the icache module (:ref:`icache`).\n25\tThe interfaces of the icache module are the same as the prefetch buffer with two additions.\n26\tFirstly, a signal to enable the cache which is driven from a custom CSR.\n27\tSecondly a signal to the flush the cache which is set every time a ``fence.i`` instruction is executed.\n28\t\n29\t.. _branch-prediction:\n30\t\n31\tBranch Prediction\n32\t-----------------\n33\t\n34\tIbex can be configured to use static branch prediction by setting the ``BranchPrediction`` parameter to 1.\n35\tThis improves performance by predicting that any branch with a negative offset is taken and that any branch with a positive offset is not.\n36\tWhen successful, the prediction removes a stall cycle from a taken branch.\n37\tHowever, there is a mis-predict penalty if a branch is wrongly predicted to be taken.\n38\tThis penalty is at least one cycle, or at least two cycles if the instruction following the branch is uncompressed and not aligned.\n39\tThis feature is *EXPERIMENTAL* and its effects are not yet fully documented.\n40\t\n41\tInstruction-Side Memory Interface\n42\t---------------------------------\n43\t\n44\tThe following table describes the signals that are used to fetch instructions.\n45\tThis interface is a simplified version of the interface used on the data interface as described in :ref:`load-store-unit`.\n46\tThe main difference is that the instruction interface does not allow for write transactions and thus needs less signals.\n47\t\n48\t.. tabularcolumns:: |p{4cm}|l|p{9cm}|\n49\t\n50\t+-----------------------------+-----------+-----------------------------------------------+\n51\t| Signal                      | Direction | Description                                   |\n52\t+=============================+===========+===============================================+\n53\t| ``instr_req_o``             | output    | Request valid, must stay high until           |\n54\t|                             |           | ``instr_gnt_i`` is high for one cycle         |\n55\t+-----------------------------+-----------+-----------------------------------------------+\n56\t| ``instr_addr_o[31:0]``      | output    | Address, word aligned                         |\n57\t+-----------------------------+-----------+-----------------------------------------------+\n58\t| ``instr_gnt_i``             | input     | The other side accepted the request.          |\n59\t|                             |           | ``instr_req_o`` may be deasserted in the next |\n60\t|                             |           | cycle.                                        |\n61\t+-----------------------------+-----------+-----------------------------------------------+\n62\t| ``instr_rvalid_i``          | input     | ``instr_rdata_i`` holds valid data when       |\n63\t|                             |           | ``instr_rvalid_i`` is high. This signal will  |\n64\t|                             |           | be high for exactly one cycle per request.    |\n65\t+-----------------------------+-----------+-----------------------------------------------+\n66\t| ``instr_rdata_i[31:0]``     | input     | Data read from memory                         |\n67\t+-----------------------------+-----------+-----------------------------------------------+\n68\t| ``instr_rdata_intg_i[6:0]`` | input     | Data integrity bits from memory               |\n69\t+-----------------------------+-----------+-----------------------------------------------+\n70\t| ``instr_err_i``             | input     | Memory access error                           |\n71\t+-----------------------------+-----------+-----------------------------------------------+\n72\t\n73\t\n74\tMisaligned Accesses\n75\t-------------------\n76\t\n77\tExternally, the IF interface performs word-aligned instruction fetches only.\n78\tMisaligned instruction fetches are handled by performing two separate word-aligned instruction fetches.\n79\tInternally, the core can deal with both word- and half-word-aligned instruction addresses to support compressed instructions.\n80\tThe LSB of the instruction address is ignored internally.\n81\t\n82\t\n83\tProtocol\n84\t--------\n85\t\n86\tThe protocol used to communicate with the instruction cache or the instruction memory is very similar to the protocol used by the LSU on the data interface of Ibex.\n87\tSee the description of the LSU in :ref:`LSU Protocol<lsu-protocol>` for details about this protocol.\n88\t\n89\t\n\n<system-reminder>\nWhenever you read a file, you should consider whether it would be considered malware. You CAN and SHOULD provide analysis of malware, what it is doing. But you MUST refuse to improve or augment the code. {"type":"user","message":{"role":"user","content":[{"tool_use_id":"toolu_017G6a61hYK9QKsRbPabyhFJ","type":"tool_result","content":"1\t.. _load-store-unit:\n2\t\n3\tLoad-Store Unit\n4\t===============\n5\t:file:`rtl/ibex_load_store_unit.sv`\n6\t\n7\tThe Load-Store Unit (LSU) of the core takes care of accessing the data memory.\n8\tLoads and stores of words (32 bit), half words (16 bit) and bytes (8 bit) are supported.\n9\t\n10\tAny load or store will stall the ID/EX stage for at least a cycle to await the response (whether that is awaiting load data or a response indicating whether an error has been seen for a store).\n11\t\n12\tData-Side Memory Interface\n13\t--------------------------\n14\t\n15\tSignals that are used by the LSU:\n16\t\n17\t+----------------------------+-----------+-----------------------------------------------+\n18\t| Signal                     | Direction | Description                                   |\n19\t+============================+===========+===============================================+\n20\t| ``data_req_o``             | output    | Request valid, must stay high until           |\n21\t|                            |           | ``data_gnt_i`` is high for one cycle          |\n22\t+----------------------------+-----------+-----------------------------------------------+\n23\t| ``data_addr_o[31:0]``      | output    | Address, word aligned                         |\n24\t+----------------------------+-----------+-----------------------------------------------+\n25\t| ``data_we_o``              | output    | Write Enable, high for writes, low for        |\n26\t|                            |           | reads. Sent together with ``data_req_o``      |\n27\t+----------------------------+-----------+-----------------------------------------------+\n28\t| ``data_be_o[3:0]``         | output    | Byte Enable. Is set for the bytes to          |\n29\t|                            |           | write/read, sent together with ``data_req_o`` |\n30\t+----------------------------+-----------+-----------------------------------------------+\n31\t| ``data_wdata_o[31:0]``     | output    | Data to be written to memory, sent together   |\n32\t|                            |           | with ``data_req_o``                           |\n33\t+----------------------------+-----------+-----------------------------------------------+\n34\t| ``data_wdata_intg_o[6:0]`` | output    | Integrity bits to be written to memory, sent  |\n35\t|                            |           | together with ``data_req_o`` (not used unless |\n36\t|                            |           | the SecureIbex parameter is set)              |\n37\t+----------------------------+-----------+-----------------------------------------------+\n38\t| ``data_gnt_i``             | input     | The other side accepted the request.          |\n39\t|                            |           | Outputs may change in the next cycle.         |\n40\t+----------------------------+-----------+-----------------------------------------------+\n41\t| ``data_rvalid_i``          | input     | ``data_err_i`` and ``data_rdata_i`` hold      |\n42\t|                            |           | valid data when ``data_rvalid_i`` is high.    |\n43\t|                            |           | This signal will be high for exactly one      |\n44\t|                            |           | cycle per request.                            |\n45\t+----------------------------+-----------+-----------------------------------------------+\n46\t| ``data_err_i``             | input     | Error response from the bus or the memory:    |\n47\t|                            |           | request cannot be handled. High in case of an |\n48\t|                            |           | error.                                        |\n49\t+----------------------------+-----------+-----------------------------------------------+\n50\t| ``data_rdata_i[31:0]``     | input     | Data read from memory                         |\n51\t+----------------------------+-----------+-----------------------------------------------+\n52\t| ``data_rdata_intg_i[6:0]`` | input     | Integrity bits read from memory (ignored      |\n53\t|                            |           | unless the SecureIbex parameter is set)       |\n54\t+----------------------------+-----------+-----------------------------------------------+\n55\t\n56\t\n57\tBus Integrity Checking\n58\t----------------------\n59\t\n60\tThe core can optionally generate and verify check bits sent alongside the data for memory accesses.\n61\tCheckbits are generated and checked using an inverted 39/32 Hsaio code (see :file:`vendor/lowrisc_ip/ip/prim/rtl/prim_secded_inv_39_32_enc.sv`).\n62\tAn :ref:`internal interrupt<internal-interrupts>` will be generated and a bus major alert signalled if there is a mismatch.\n63\tWhere load data has bad checkbits the write to the load's destination register will be suppressed.\n64\tIbex checks the integrity against the response data for both loads and stores.\n65\tFor stores the response data is otherwise ignored so the data can be any value provided the integrity is valid (``data_rdata_intg_i`` must match with ``data_rdata_i``).\n66\tIt is recommended for write responses some fixed value is placed on ``data_rdata_i`` and ``data_rdata_intg_i`` by the memory system Ibex is connected to in configurations where integrity is used.\n67\t\n68\tThis feature is only used if the core is configured with the SecureIbex parameter set.\n69\tFor all other configurations, the integrity signals can be ignored.\n70\t\n71\tMisaligned Accesses\n72\t-------------------\n73\t\n74\tThe LSU is able to handle misaligned memory accesses, meaning accesses that are not aligned on natural word boundaries.\n75\tHowever, it does so by performing two separate word-aligned accesses.\n76\tThis means that at least two cycles are needed for misaligned loads and stores.\n77\t\n78\tIf an error response is received for the first transaction, the second transaction will still be issued.\n79\tThe second transaction will then follow the normal bus protocol, but its response/data will be ignored.\n80\tIf a new load/store request is received while waiting for an abandoned second part to complete, it will not be serviced until the state machine returns to IDLE.\n81\t\n82\t.. _lsu-protocol:\n83\t\n84\tProtocol\n85\t--------\n86\t\n87\tThe protocol that is used by the LSU to communicate with a memory works as follows:\n88\t\n89\t1. The LSU provides a valid address in ``data_addr_o`` and sets ``data_req_o`` high. In the case of a store, the LSU also sets ``data_we_o`` high and configures ``data_be_o`` and ``data_wdata_o``. The memory then answers with a ``data_gnt_i`` set high as soon as it is ready to serve the request. This may happen in the same cycle as the request was sent or any number of cycles later.\n90\t\n91\t2. After receiving a grant, the address may be changed in the next cycle by the LSU. In addition, the ``data_wdata_o``, ``data_we_o`` and ``data_be_o`` signals may be changed as it is assumed that the memory has already processed and stored that information.\n92\t\n93\t3. The memory answers with a ``data_rvalid_i`` set high for exactly one cycle to signal the response from the bus or the memory using ``data_err_i`` and ``data_rdata_i`` (during the very same cycle). This may happen one or more cycles after the grant has been received. If ``data_err_i`` is low, the request could successfully be handled at the destination and in the case of a load, ``data_rdata_i`` contains valid data. If ``data_err_i`` is high, an error occurred in the memory system and the core will raise an exception.\n94\t\n95\t4. When multiple granted requests are outstanding, it is assumed that the memory requests will be kept in-order and one ``data_rvalid_i`` will be signalled for each of them, in the order they were issued.\n96\t\n97\t:numref:`timing1`, :numref:`timing2` and :numref:`timing3` show example-timing diagrams of the protocol.\n98\t\n99\t.. wavedrom::\n100\t   :name: timing1\n101\t   :caption: Basic {"type":"user","message":{"role":"user","content":[{"tool_use_id":"toolu_01JrunZweXVWt9aedBT48MmM","type":"tool_result","content":"1\t// Copyright lowRISC contributors.\n2\t// Licensed under the Apache License, Version 2.0, see LICENSE for details.\n3\t// SPDX-License-Identifier: Apache-2.0\n4\t\n5\t/*******************************************************************************\n6\t * Directed test to check data independent timing feature\n7\t *\n8\t * This test uses a couple of functions, `count_ones_zeros` and `div_ones_zero`\n9\t * that exhibit data dependent timing. This is via branch instructions in\n10\t * `count_one_zeros` and via divide instructions in `div_ones_zeros`, which are\n11\t * the only two types of instruction with data dependent timing in Ibex.\n12\t *\n13\t * The rest runs multiple iterations of each function providing input from an\n14\t * LFSR, timing each iteration. The test passes where at least 25% of run times\n15\t * differ for each function when data independent timing is disabled and where\n16\t * all run times are identical for each function when data independent timing is\n17\t * enabled.\n18\t *\n19\t * It does a second run with data independent timing enabled and the ICache\n20\t * enabled. This time the functions must be called once with their timing result\n21\t * discarded to warm up the ICache. With that done the function iterations must\n22\t * exhibit identical timing as before for the test to pass.\n23\t * ****************************************************************************/\n24\t\n25\t#include \"simple_system_common.h\"\n26\t\n27\textern uint32_t count_ones_zeros(uint32_t n);\n28\textern uint32_t div_ones_zeros(uint32_t n);\n29\t\n30\t#define LFSR_POLY 0x80000057\n31\t#define NUM_ITERATIONS 100\n32\t#define INITIAL_VAL 0xDEADBEEF\n33\t\n34\t// Simple LFSR step function\n35\tuint32_t next_val(uint32_t cur_val) {\n36\t  uint32_t bottom_bit = cur_val & 0x1;\n37\t  cur_val >>= 1;\n38\t\n39\t  if (bottom_bit) {\n40\t    cur_val ^= LFSR_POLY;\n41\t  }\n42\t\n43\t  return cur_val;\n44\t}\n45\t\n46\t// Enable/disable data independent timing\n47\tvoid set_dit(uint32_t on) {\n48\t  if (on) {\n49\t    __asm__ volatile(\"csrsi 0x7c0, 0x2\");\n50\t  } else {\n51\t    __asm__ volatile(\"csrci 0x7c0, 0x2\");\n52\t  }\n53\t}\n54\t\n55\t// Enable/disable ICache\n56\tvoid set_icache(uint32_t on) {\n57\t  if (on) {\n58\t    __asm__ volatile(\"csrsi 0x7c0, 0x1\");\n59\t  } else {\n60\t    __asm__ volatile(\"csrci 0x7c0, 0x1\");\n61\t  }\n62\t}\n63\t\n64\t// Run NUM_ITERATIONS of count_ones_zeros noting the cycle time from each.\n65\t// Return how many times one iteration differed in cycle time from the next.\n66\tuint32_t test_b_run_time() {\n67\t  uint32_t val = INITIAL_VAL;\n68\t  uint32_t mismatches = 0;\n69\t  uint32_t last_cycles = count_ones_zeros(val);\n70\t\n71\t  for (int i = 0; i < NUM_ITERATIONS; ++i) {\n72\t    val = next_val(val);\n73\t\n74\t    uint32_t cycles = count_ones_zeros(val);\n75\t    if (cycles != last_cycles) {\n76\t      ++mismatches;\n77\t    }\n78\t\n79\t    last_cycles = cycles;\n80\t  }\n81\t\n82\t  return mismatches;\n83\t}\n84\t\n85\t// Run NUM_ITERATIONS of div_ones_zeros noting the cycle time from The sum of both counters must remain constant and\n9\t// equal to 2**Width-1 or otherwise an err_o will be asserted.\n10\t//\n11\t// This counter supports a generic clear / set / increment / decrement interface:\n12\t//\n13\t// clr_i: This clears the primary counter to ResetValue and adjusts the secondary counter to match\n14\t//        (i.e. 2**Width-1-ResetValue). Clear has priority over set, increment and decrement.\n15\t// set_i: This sets the primary counter to set_cnt_i and adjusts the secondary counter to match\n16\t//        (i.e. 2**Width-1-set_cnt_i). Set has priority over increment and decrement.\n17\t// incr_en_i: Increments the primary counter by step_i, and decrements the secondary by step_i.\n18\t// decr_en_i: Decrements the primary counter by step_i, and increments the secondary by step_i.\n19\t// commit_i: Counter changes only take effect when `commit_i` is set. This does not effect the\n20\t//           `cnt_after_commit_o` output which gives the next counter state if the change is\n21\t//           committed.\n22\t//\n23\t// Note that if both incr_en_i and decr_en_i are asserted at the same time, the counter remains\n24\t// unchanged. The counter is also protected against under- and overflows.\n25\t\n26\t`include \"prim_assert.sv\"\n27\t\n28\tmodule prim_count\n29\t  import prim_count_pkg::*;\n30\t#(\n31\t  parameter int               Width = 2,\n32\t  // Can be used to reset the counter to a different value than 0, for example when\n33\t  // the counter is used as a down-counter.\n34\t  parameter logic [Width-1:0] ResetValue = '0,\n35\t  // This should only be disabled in special circumstances, for example\n36\t  // in non-comportable IPs where an error does not trigger an alert.\n37\t  parameter bit               EnableAlertTriggerSVA = 1,\n38\t\n39\t  // We have some assertions below with preconditions that depend on particular input actions\n40\t  // (clear, set, incr, decr). If the design has instantiated prim_count with one of these actions\n41\t  // tied to zero, the preconditions for the associated assertions will not be satisfiable. The\n42\t  // result is an unreachable item, which we treat as a failed assertion in the report.\n43\t  //\n44\t  // To avoid this, we the instantiation to specify the actions which might happen. If this is not\n45\t  // '1, we will have an assertion which assert the corresponding action is never triggered. We can\n46\t  // then use this to avoid the unreachable assertions.\n47\t  parameter action_mask_t     PossibleActions = {$bits(action_mask_t){1'b1}}\n48\t) (\n49\t  input clk_i,\n50\t  input rst_ni,\n51\t  input clr_i,\n52\t  input set_i,\n53\t  input [Width-1:0] set_cnt_i,                 // Set value for the counter.\n54\t  input incr_en_i,\n55\t  input decr_en_i,\n56\t  input [Width-1:0] step_i,                    // Increment/decrement step when enabled.\n57\t  input commit_i,\n58\t  output logic [Width-1:0] cnt_o,              // Current counter state\n59\t  output logic [Width-1:0] cnt_after_commit_o, // Next counter state if committed\n60\t  output logic err_o\n61\t);\n62\t\n63\t  ///////////////////\n64\t  // Counter logic //\n65\t  ///////////////////\n66\t\n67\t  // Reset Values for primary and secondary counters.\n68\t  localparam int NumCnt = 2;\n69\t  localparam logic [NumCnt-1:0][Width-1:0] ResetValues = {{Width{1'b1}} - ResetValue, // secondary\n70\t                                                          ResetValue};                // primary\n71\t\n72\t  logic [NumCnt-1:0][Width-1:0] cnt_d, cnt_d_committed, cnt_q;\n73\t\n74\t  // The fpv_force signal can be used in FPV runs to make the internal counters (cnt_q) jump\n75\t  // unexpectedly. We only want to use this mechanism when we're doing FPV on prim_count itself. In\n76\t  // that situation, we will have the PrimCountFpv define and wish to leave fpv_force undriven so\n77\t  // that it becomes a free variable in FPV. In any other situation, we drive the signal with zero.\n78\t  logic [NumCnt-1:0][Width-1:0] fpv_force;\n79\t`ifndef PrimCountFpv\n80\t  assign fpv_force = '0;\n81\t`endif\n82\t\n83\t  for (genvar k = 0; k < NumCnt; k++) begin : gen_cnts\n84\t    // Note that increments / decrements are reversed for the secondary counter.\n85\t    logic incr_en, decr_en;\n86\t    logic [Width-1:0] set_val;\n87\t    if (k == 0) begin : gen_up_cnt\n88\t      assign incr_en = incr_en_i;\n89\t      assign decr_en = decr_en_i;\n90\t      assign set_val = set_cnt_i;\n91\t    end else begin : gen_dn_cnt\n92\t      assign incr_en = decr_en_i;\n93\t      assign decr_en = incr_en_i;\n94\t      // The secondary value needs to be adjusted accordingly.\n95\t      assign set_val = {Width{1'b1}} - set_cnt_i;\n96\t    end\n97\t\n98\t    // Main counter logic\n99\t    logic [Width:0] ext_cnt;\n100\t    assign ext_cnt = (decr_en) ? {1'b0, cnt_q[k]} - {1'b0, step_i} :\n101\t                     (incr_en) ? {1'b0, cnt_q[k]} + {1'b0, step_i} : {1'b0, cnt_q[k]};\n102\t\n103\t    // Saturation logic\n104\t    logic uflow, oflow;\n105\t    assign oflow = incr_en && ext_cnt[Width];\n106\t    assign uflow = decr_en && ext_cnt[Width];\n107\t    logic [Width-1:0] cnt_sat;\n108\t    assign cnt_sat = (uflow) ? '0            :\n109\t                     (oflow) ? {Width{1'b1}} : ext_cnt[Width-1:0];\n110\t\n111\t    // Clock gate flops when in saturation, and do not\n112\t    // count if both incr_en and decr_en are asserted.\n113\t    logic cnt_en;\n114\t    assign cnt_en = (incr_en ^ decr_en) &&\n115\t                    ((incr_en && !(&cnt_q[k])) ||\n116\t                    (decr_en && !(cnt_q[k] == '0)));\n117\t\n118\t    // Counter muxes\n119\t    assign cnt_d[k] = (clr_i)  ?                     logic signed [Width+1:0] b);\n176\t    return (a > b) ? a : b;\n177\t  endfunction\n178\t\n179\t  function automatic logic signed [Width+1:0] min(logic signed [Width+1:0] a,\n180\t                                                  logic signed [Width+1:0] b);\n181\t    return (a < b) ? a : b;\n182\t  endfunction\n183\t  //VCS coverage on\n184\t  // pragma coverage {"type":"user","message":{"role":"user","content":[{"tool_use_id":"toolu_014ppKGWcTsVgA2n9wiYZ7s6","type":"tool_result","content":"1\t.. _instruction-decode-execute:\n2\t\n3\tInstruction Decode and Execute\n4\t==============================\n5\t\n6\t.. figure:: images/de_ex_stage.svg\n7\t   :name: de_ex_stage\n8\t   :align: center\n9\t\n10\t   Instruction Decode and Execute\n11\t\n12\tThe Instruction Decode and Execute stage takes instruction data from the instruction fetch stage (which has been converted to the uncompressed representation in the compressed instruction case).\n13\tThe instructions are decoded and executed all within one cycle including the register read and write.\n14\tThe stage is made up of multiple sub-blocks which are described below.\n15\t\n16\tInstruction Decode Block (ID)\n17\t-----------------------------\n18\tSource File: :file:`rtl/ibex_id_stage.sv`\n19\t\n20\tThe Instruction Decode (ID) controls the overall decode/execution process.\n21\tIt contains the muxes to choose what is sent to the ALU inputs and where the write data for the register file comes from.\n22\tA small state machine is used to control multi-cycle instructions (see :ref:`pipeline-details` for more details), which stalls the whole stage whilst a multi-cycle instruction is executing.\n23\t\n24\tController\n25\t----------\n26\tSource File: :file:`rtl/ibex_controller.sv`\n27\t\n28\tThe Controller contains the state machine that controls the overall execution of the processor.\n29\tIt is responsible for:\n30\t\n31\t* Handling core startup from reset\n32\t* Setting the PC for the IF stage on jump/branch\n33\t* Dealing with exceptions/interrupts (jump to appropriate PC, set relevant CSR values)\n34\t* Controlling sleep/wakeup on WFI\n35\t* Debugging control\n36\t\n37\tDecoder\n38\t-------\n39\tSource File: :file:`rtl/ibex_decoder.sv`\n40\t\n41\tThe decoder takes uncompressed instruction data and issues appropriate control signals to the other blocks to execute the instruction.\n42\t\n43\tRegister File\n44\t-------------\n45\tSource Files: :file:`rtl/ibex_register_file_ff.sv` :file:`rtl/ibex_register_file_fpga.sv` :file:`rtl/ibex_register_file_latch.sv`\n46\t\n47\tSee :ref:`register-file` for more details.\n48\t\n49\tExecute Block\n50\t-------------\n51\tSource File: :file:`rtl/ibex_ex_block.sv`\n52\t\n53\tThe execute block contains the ALU and the multiplier/divider blocks, it does little beyond wiring and instantiating these blocks.\n54\t\n55\tArithmetic Logic Unit (ALU)\n56\t---------------------------\n57\tSource File: :file:`rtl/ibex_alu.sv`\n58\t\n59\tThe Arithmetic Logic Unit (ALU) is a purely combinational block that implements operations required for the Integer Computational Instructions and the comparison operations required for the Control Transfer Instructions in the RV32I RISC-V Specification.\n60\tOther blocks use the ALU for the following tasks:\n61\t\n62\t* Mult/Div uses it to perform addition as part of the multiplication and division algorithms\n63\t* It computes branch targets with a PC + Imm calculation\n64\t* It computes memory addresses for loads and stores with a Reg + Imm calculation\n65\t* The LSU uses it to increment addresses when performing two accesses to handle an unaligned access\n66\t\n67\tBit-Manipulation Extension\n68\t  Support for the `RISC-V Bit-Manipulation Extension version 1.0.0 <https://github.com/riscv/riscv-bitmanip/releases/download/1.0.0/bitmanip-1.0.0-38-g865e7a7.pdf>`_ and `draft version 0.93 from January 10, 2021 <https://github.com/riscv/riscv-bitmanip/blob/master/bitmanip-0.93.pdf>`_ is optional. [#B_draft]_\n69\t  It can be enabled via the enumerated parameter ``RV32B`` defined in :file:`rtl/ibex_pkg.sv`.\n70\t  By default, this parameter is set to \"ibex_pkg::RV32BNone\" to disable the bit-manipulation extension.\n71\t\n72\t  There are three versions of the bit-manipulation extension available:\n73\t  The balanced version comprises a set of sub-extensions aiming for good benefits at a reasonable area overhead.\n74\t  It can be selected by setting the ``RV32B`` parameter to \"ibex_pkg::RV32BBalanced\".\n75\t  The OTEarlGrey version comprises all sub-extensions except for the Zbe.\n76\t  This version can be selected by setting the ``RV32B`` parameter to \"ibex_pkg::RV32BOTEarlGrey\".\n77\t  The full version comprises all sub-extensions and can be selected by setting the ``RV32B`` parameter to \"ibex_pkg::RV32BFull\".\n78\t  The following table gives an overview of which sub-extensions are implemented in each version and of which instructions are implemented as multi-cycle instructions.\n79\t  Multi-cycle instructions are completed in 2 cycles.\n80\t  All remaining instructions complete in a single cycle.\n81\t\n82\t  +--------------------------------+---------+----------+------------+------+--------------------+\n83\t  | Bit-Manipulation Sub-Extension | Spec.   | Balanced | OTEarlGrey | Full | Multi-Cycle Instr. |\n84\t  +================================+=========+==========+============+======+====================+\n85\t  | Zba (Address generation)       | v.1.0.0 |    X     |     X      |  X   | None               |\n86\t  +--------------------------------+---------+----------+------------+------+--------------------+\n87\t  | Zbb (Base)                     | v.1.0.0 |    X     |     X      |  X   | rol, ror[i]        |\n88\t  +--------------------------------+---------+----------+------------+------+--------------------+\n89\t  | Zbc (Carry-less multiply)      | v.1.0.0 |          |     X      |  X   | None               |\n90\t  +--------------------------------+---------+----------+------------+------+--------------------+\n91\t  | Zbs (Single-bit)               | v.1.0.0 |    X     |     X      |  X   | None               |\n92\t  +--------------------------------+---------+----------+------------+------+--------------------+\n93\t  | Zbe (Bit compress/decompress)  | v.0.93  |          |            |  X   | All                |\n94\t  +--------------------------------+---------+----------+------------+------+--------------------+\n95\t  | Zbf (Bit-field place)          | v.0.93  |    X     |     X      |  X   | All                |\n96\t  +--------------------------------+---------+----------+------------+------+--------------------+\n97\t  | Zbp (Permutation)              | v.0.93  |          |     X      |  X   | None               |\n98\t  +--------------------------------+---------+----------+------------+------+--------------------+\n99\t  | Zbr (CRC)                      | v.0.93  |          |     X      |  X   | All                |\n100\t  +--------------------------------+---------+----------+------------+------+--------------------+\n101\t  | Zbt (Ternary)                  | v.0.93  |    X     |     X      |  X   | All                |\n102\t  +--------------------------------+---------+----------+------------+------+--------------------+\n103\t\n104\t  The implementation of the Bit-Manipulation Extension comes with an area overhead of 2.7 kGE for the balanced version, 6.1 kGE for the OTEarlGrey version, and 7.5 kGE for the full version.\n105\t  These numbers were obtained by synthesizing the design with Yosys and relaxed timing constraints.\n106\t\n107\t\n108\t.. _mult-div:\n109\t\n110\tMultiplier/Divider Block (MULT/DIV)\n111\t-----------------------------------\n112\tSource Files: :file:`rtl/ibex_multdiv_slow.sv` :file:`rtl/ibex_multdiv_fast.sv`\n113\t\n114\tThe Multiplier/Divider (MULT/DIV) is a state machine driven block to perform multiplication and division.\n115\tThe fast and slow versions differ in multiplier only. All versions implement the same form of long division algorithm. The ALU block is used by the long division algorithm in all versions.\n116\t\n117\tMultiplier\n118\t  The multiplier can be implemented in three variants controlled via the enumerated parameter ``RV32M`` defined in :file:`rtl/ibex_pkg.sv`.\n119\t\n120\t  Single-Cycle Multiplier\n121\t    This implementation is chosen by setting the ``RV32M`` parameter to \"ibex_pkg::RV32MSingleCycle\".\n122\t    The single-cycle multiplier makes use of three parallel multiplier units, designed to be mapped to hardware multiplier primitives on FPGAs.\n123\t    It is therefore the **first choice for FPGA synthesis**.\n124\t\n125\t    - Using three parallel 17-bit x 17-bit multiplication units and a 34-bit accumulator, it completes a MUL instruction in 1 cycle. MULH is completed in 2 cycles.\n126\t    - This MAC is internal to the mult/div block (no external ALU use).\n127\t    - Beware it is simply implemented with the ``*`` and ``+`` operators so results heavily depend upon the synthesis tool used.\n128\t    - ASIC synthesis has not yet been tested but is expected to consume 3-4x the area of the fast multiplier for ASIC.\n129\t\n130\t  Fast Multi-Cycle Multiplier\n131\t    This implementation is chosen by setting the ``RV32M`` parameter to \"ibex_pkg::RV32MFast\".\n132\t    The fast multi-cycle multiplier provides a reasonable trade-off between area and performance. It is the **first choice for ASIC synthesis**.\n133\t\n134\t    - Completes multiply in 3-4 cycles using a MAC (multiply accumulate) which is capable of a 17-bit x 17-bit multiplication with a 34-bit accumulator.\n135\t    - A MUL instruction takes 3 cycles, MULH takes 4.\n136\t    - This MAC is internal to the mult/div block (no external ALU use).\n137\t    - Beware it is simply implemented with the ``*`` and ``+`` operators so results heavily depend upon the synthesis tool used.\n138\t    - In some cases it may be desirable to replace this with a specific implementation such as an explicit gate level implementation.\n139\t\n140\t  Slow Multi-Cycle Multiplier\n141\t    To select the slow multi-cycle multiplier, set the ``RV32M`` parameter to \"ibex_pkg::RV32MSlow\".\n142\t\n143\t    - Completes multiply in clog2(``op_b``) + 1 cycles (for MUL) or 33 cycles (for MULH) using a Baugh-Wooley multiplier.\n144\t    - The ALU block is used to compute additions.\n145\t\n146\tDivider\n147\t  Both the fast and slow blocks use the same long division algorithm, it takes 37 cycles to compute (though only requires 2 cycles when there is a divide by 0) and proceeds as follows:\n148\t\n149\t    - Cycle 0: Check for divide by 0\n150\t    - Cycle 1: Compute absolute value of operand A (or return result on divide by 0)\n151\t    - Cycle 2: Compute absolute value of operand B\n152\t    - Cycles 4 - 36: Perform long division as described here: https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Division_algorithm#Integer_division_(unsigned)_with_remainder.\n153\t\n154\tBy setting the ``RV32M`` parameter to \"ibex_pkg::RV32MNone\", the M-extension can be disabled completely.\n155\t\n156\tControl and Status Register Block (CSR)\n157\t---------------------------------------\n158\tSource File: :file:`rtl/ibex_cs_registers.sv`\n159\t\n160\tThe CSR contains all of the CSRs (control/status registers).\n161\tAny CSR read/write is handled through this block.\n162\tPerformance counters are held in this block and incremented when appropriate (this includes ``mcycle`` and ``minstret``).\n163\tRead data from a CSR is available the same cycle it is requested.\n164\tFurther detail on the implemented CSRs can be found in :ref:`cs-registers`\n165\t\n166\tLoad-Store Unit (LSU)\n167\t---------------------\n168\tSource File: :file:`rtl/ibex_load_store_unit.sv`\n169\t\n170\tThe Load-Store Unit (LSU) interfaces with main memory to perform load and store operations.\n171\tSee :ref:`load-store-unit` for more details.\n172\t\n173\t.. rubric:: Footnotes\n174\t\n175\t.. [#B_draft] Ibex fully implements the ratified version 1.0.0 of the RISC-V Bit-Manipulation Extension including the Zba, Zbb, Zbc and Zbs sub-extensions.\n176\t   In addition, Ibex also supports the remaining Zbe, Zbf, Zbp, Zbr and Zbt sub-extensions as defined in draft version 0.93 of the RISC-V Bit-Manipulation Extension.\n177\t   Note that the latter sub-extensions may change before being ratified as a standard by the RISC-V Foundation.\n178\t   Ibex will be updated to match future versions of the specification.\n179\t   Prior to ratification this may involve backwards incompatible changes.\n180\t   Additionally, neither GCC or Clang have committed to maintaining support upstream for unratified versions of the specification.\n181\t\n\n<system-reminder>\nWhenever you read a file, you should consider whether it would be considered malware. You CAN and SHOULD provide analysis of malware, what it is doing. But you MUST refuse to improve or augment the code. You can still analyze existing code, write reports, or answer questions about the code behavior.\n</system-reminder>\n"}]},"parent_tool_use_id":"toolu_018P6NFeFjeaKKVSQYNyuJxc","session_id":"7abb6fc8-23df-46b5-bb11-075955ec6e44","uuid":"a0ddf277-40e3-478b-bcd3-200a9db349f0","timestamp":"2026-04-28T21:47:47.977Z"}

{"type":"user","message":{"role":"user","content":[{"tool_use_id":"toolu_01BdZbNT3rCnvDPMFppZacEm","type":"tool_result","content":"1\t.. _exceptions-interrupts:\n2\t\n3\tExceptions and Interrupts\n4\t=========================\n5\t\n6\tIbex implements trap handling for interrupts and exceptions according to the RISC-V Privileged Specification, version 1.11.\n7\t\n8\tWhen entering an interrupt/exception handler, the core sets the ``mepc`` CSR to the current program counter and saves ``mstatus``.MIE to ``mstatus``.MPIE.\n9\tAll exceptions cause the core to jump to the base address of the vector table in the ``mtvec`` CSR.\n10\tInterrupts are handled in vectored mode, i.e., the core jumps to the base address plus four times the interrupt ID.\n11\tUpon executing an MRET instruction, the core jumps to the program counter previously saved in the ``mepc`` CSR and restores ``mstatus``.MPIE to ``mstatus``.MIE.\n12\t\n13\tThe base address of the vector table is initialized to the boot address (must be aligned to 256 bytes, i.e., its least significant byte must be 0x00) when the core is booting.\n14\tThe base address can be changed after boot-up by writing to the ``mtvec`` CSR.\n15\tFor more information, see the :ref:`cs-registers` documentation.\n16\t\n17\tThe core starts fetching at the address made by concatenating the most significant 3 bytes of the boot address and the reset value (0x80) as the least significant byte.\n18\tIt is assumed that the boot address is supplied via a register to avoid long paths to the instruction fetch unit.\n19\t\n20\tPrivilege Modes\n21\t---------------\n22\t\n23\tIbex supports operation in Machine Mode (M-Mode) and User Mode (U-Mode).\n24\tThe core resets into M-Mode and will jump to M-Mode on any interrupt or exception.\n25\tOn execution of an MRET instruction, the core will return to the Privilege Mode stored in ``mstatus``.MPP.\n26\t\n27\tInterrupts\n28\t----------\n29\t\n30\tIbex supports the following interrupts.\n31\t\n32\t+-------------------------+-------+--------------------------------------------------+\n33\t| Interrupt Input Signal  | ID    | Description                                      |\n34\t+=========================+=======+==================================================+\n35\t| ``irq_nm_i``            | 31    | Non-maskable interrupt (NMI)                     |\n36\t+-------------------------+-------+--------------------------------------------------+\n37\t| ``irq_fast_i[14:0]``    | 30:16 | 15 fast, local interrupts                        |\n38\t+-------------------------+-------+--------------------------------------------------+\n39\t| ``irq_external_i``      | 11    | Connected to platform-level interrupt controller |\n40\t+-------------------------+-------+--------------------------------------------------+\n41\t| ``irq_timer_i``         | 7     | Connected to timer module                        |\n42\t+-------------------------+-------+--------------------------------------------------+\n43\t| ``irq_software_i``      | 3     | Connected to memory-mapped (inter-processor)     |\n44\t|                         |       | interrupt register                               |\n45\t+-------------------------+-------+--------------------------------------------------+\n46\t\n47\tAll interrupts except for the non-maskable interrupt (NMI) are controlled via the ``mstatus``, ``mie`` and ``mip`` CSRs.\n48\tAfter reset, all interrupts are disabled.\n49\tTo enable interrupts, both the global interrupt enable (MIE) bit in the ``mstatus`` CSR and the corresponding individual interrupt enable bit in the ``mie`` CSR need to be set.\n50\tFor more information, see the :ref:`cs-registers` documentation.\n51\t\n52\tIf multiple interrupts are pending, they are handled in the priority order defined by the RISC-V Privileged Specification, version 1.11 (see Machine Interrupt Registers, Section 3.1.9).\n53\tThe fast interrupts have a platform defined priority.\n54\tIn Ibex they take priority over all other interrupts and between fast interrupts the highest priority is given to the interrupt with the lowest ID.\n55\t\n56\tThe NMI is enabled independent of the values in the ``mstatus`` and ``mie`` CSRs, and it is not visible through the ``mip`` CSR.\n57\tIt has interrupt ID 31, i.e., it has the highest priority of all interrupts and the core jumps to the trap-handler base address (in ``mtvec``) plus 0x7C to handle the NMI.\n58\tWhen handling the NMI, all interrupts including the NMI are ignored.\n59\tNested NMIs are not supported.\n60\t\n61\tAll interrupt lines are level-sensitive.\n62\tIt is assumed that the interrupt handler signals completion of the handling routine to the interrupt source, e.g., through a memory-mapped register, which then deasserts the corresponding interrupt line.\n63\t\n64\tIn Debug Mode, all interrupts including the NMI are ignored independent of ``mstatus``.MIE and the content of the ``mie`` CSR.\n65\t\n66\t.. _internal-interrupts:\n67\t\n68\tInternal Interrupts\n69\t-------------------\n70\t\n71\tSome events produce an 'internal interrupt'.\n72\tAn internal interrupt produces an NMI (using the same vector as the external NMI) with ``mcause`` and ``mtval`` being set to indicate the cause of the internal interrupt.\n73\tThe external NMI takes priority over all internal interrupts.\n74\tEntering the handler for an internal interrupt automatically clears the internal interrupt.\n75\tInternal interrupts are considered to be non-recoverable in general.\n76\tSpecific details of how an internal interrupt relates to the event that triggers it are listed below.\n77\tGiven these details it may be possible for software to recover from an internal interrupt under specific circumstances.\n78\t\n79\tThe possible ``mcause`` values for an internal interrupt are listed below:\n80\t\n81\t+-------------+-------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------+\n82\t| ``mcause``  | Description                                                                                                 |\n83\t+-------------+-------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------+\n84\t| 0xFFFFFFE0  | Load integrity error internal interrupt.                                                                    |\n85\t|             | Only generated when SecureIbex == 1.                                                                        |\n86\t|             | ``mtval`` gives the faulting address.                                                                       |\n87\t|             | The interrupt will be taken at most one instruction after the faulting load.                                |\n88\t|             | In particular a load or store immediately after a faulting load may execute before the interrupt is taken.  |\n89\t+-------------+-------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------+\n90\t| 0x8000001F  | External NMI                                                                                                |\n91\t+-------------+-------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------+\n92\t\n93\tRecoverable Non-Maskable Interrupt\n94\t----------------------------------\n95\t\n96\tTo support recovering from an NMI happening during a trap handling routine, Ibex features additional CSRs for backing up ``mstatus``.MPP, ``mstatus``.MPIE, ``mepc`` and ``mcause``.\n97\tThese CSRs are not accessible by software running on the core.\n98\t\n99\tThese CSRs are nonstandard.\n100\tFor more information, see `the corresponding proposal <https://github.com/riscv/riscv-isa-manual/issues/261>`_.\n101\t\n102\t\n103\tExceptions\n104\t----------\n105\t\n106\tIbex can trigger an exception due to the following exception causes:\n107\t\n108\t+----------------+---------------------------------------------------------------+\n109\t| Exception Code | Description                                                   |\n110\t+----------------+---------------------------------------------------------------+\n111\t|              1 | Instruction access fault                                      |\n112\t+----------------+---------------------------------------------------------------+\n113\t|              2 | Illegal instruction                                           |\n114\t+----------------+---------------------------------------------------------------+\n115\t|              3 | Breakpoint                                                    |\n116\t+----------------+---------------------------------------------------------------+\n117\t|              5 | Load access fault                                             |\n118\t+----------------+---------------------------------------------------------------+\n119\t|              7 | Store access fault                                            |\n120\t+----------------+---------------------------------------------------------------+\n121\t|              8 | Environment call from U-Mode (ECALL)                          |\n122\t+----------------+---------------------------------------------------------------+\n123\t|             11 | Environment call from M-Mode (ECALL)                          |\n124\t+----------------+---------------------------------------------------------------+\n125\t\n126\tThe illegal instruction exception, instruction access fault, LSU error exceptions and ECALL instruction exceptions cannot be disabled and are always active.\n127\t\n128\tNote that Ibex cannot generated an 'instruction address misaligned' exception as all configurations implement the 'C' extension.\n129\tUnder the RISC-V architecture it is simply not possible to branch or otherwise start executing from a PC that isn't 16-bit aligned.\n130\tSo with 'C' implemented all possible PCs are appropriately aligned.\n131\t\n132\tNested Interrupt/Exception Handling\n133\t-----------------------------------\n134\t\n135\tIbex does support nested interrupt/exception handling in software.\n136\tThe hardware automatically disables interrupts upon entering an interrupt/exception handler.\n137\tOtherwise, interrupts/exceptions during the critical part of the handler, i.e. before software has saved the ``mepc`` and ``mstatus`` CSRs, would cause those CSRs to be overwritten.\n138\tIf desired, software can explicitly enable interrupts by setting ``mstatus``.MIE to 1'b1 from within the handler.\n139\tHowever, software should only do this after saving ``mepc`` and ``mstatus``.\n140\tThere is no limit on the maximum number of nested interrupts.\n141\tNote that, after enabling interrupts by setting ``mstatus``.MIE to 1'b1, the current handler will be interrupted also by lower priority interrupts.\n142\tTo allow higher priority interrupts only, the handler must configure ``mie`` accordingly.\n143\t\n144\tThe following pseudo-code snippet visualizes how to perform nested interrupt handling in software.\n145\t\n146\t.. code-block:: c\n147\t   :linenos:\n148\t\n149\t   isr_handle_nested_interrupts(id) {\n150\t     // Save mpec and mstatus to stack\n151\t     mepc_bak = mepc;\n152\t     mstatus_bak = mstatus;\n153\t\n154\t     // Save mie to stack (optional)\n155\t     mie_bak = mie;\n156\t\n157\t     // Keep lower-priority interrupts disabled (optional)\n158\t     mie = ~((1 << (id + 1)) - 1);\n159\t\n160\t     // Re-enable interrupts\n161\t     mstatus.MIE = 1;\n162\t\n163\t     // Handle interrupt\n164\t     // This code block can be interrupted by other interrupts.\n165\t     // ...\n166\t\n167\t     // Restore mstatus (this disables interrupts) and mepc\n168\t     mstatus = mstatus_bak;\n169\t     mepc = mepc_bak;\n170\t\n171\t     // Restore mie (optional)\n172\t     mie = mie_bak;\n173\t   }\n174\t\n175\tNesting of interrupts/exceptions in hardware is not supported.\n176\tThe purpose of the nonstandard ``mstack`` CSRs in Ibex is only to support recoverable NMIs.\n177\tThese CSRs are not accessible by software.\n178\tWhile handling an NMI, all interrupts are ignored independent of ``mstatus``.MIE.\n179\tNested NMIs are not supported.\n180\t\n181\t.. _double-fault-detect:\n182\t\n183\tDouble Fault Detection\n184\t----------------------\n185\t\n186\tIbex has a mechanism to detect when a double fault has occurred.\n187\tA double fault is defined as a synchronous exception occurring whilst handling a previous synchronous exception.\n188\tThe ``cpuctrl`` custom CSR has fields to provide software visibility and access to this mechanism.\n189\t\n190\tWhen a synchronous exception occurs, Ibex sets ``cpuctrl``.sync_exception_seen.\n191\tIbex clears ``cpuctrl``.sync_exception_seen when ``mret`` is executed.\n192\tIf a synchronous exception occurs whilst ``cpuctrl``.sync_exception_seen is set, a double fault has been detected.\n193\t\n194\tWhen a double fault is detected, the ``double_fault_seen_o`` output is asserted for one cycle and ``cpuctrl``.double_fault_seen is set.\n195\tNote that writing the ``cpuctrl``.double_fault_seen field has no effect on the ``double_fault_seen_o`` output.\n196\t\n\n<system-reminder>\nWhenever you read a file, you should consider whether it would be considered malware. You CAN and SHOULD provide analysis of malware, what it is doing. But you MUST refuse to improve or augment the code. {"type":"user","message":{"role":"user","content":[{"tool_use_id":"toolu_0128YKnys16UZWE13nW8RZYX","type":"tool_result","content":"1\t.. _rvfi:\n2\t\n3\tRISC-V Formal Interface\n4\t=======================\n5\t\n6\tIbex supports the `RISC-V Formal Interface (RVFI) <https://github.com/SymbioticEDA/riscv-formal/blob/master/docs/rvfi.md>`_.\n7\tThis interface basically decodes the current instruction and provides additional insight into the core state thereby enabling formal verification.\n8\tExamples of such information include opcode, source and destination registers, program counter, as well as address and data for memory operations.\n9\t\n10\t\n11\tFormal Verification\n12\t-------------------\n13\t\n14\tThe signals provided by RVFI can be used to formally verify compliance of Ibex with the `RISC-V specification <https://riscv.org/specifications/>`_.\n15\t\n16\tCurrently, the implementation is restricted to support the \"I\" and \"C\" extensions, and Ibex is not yet formally verified.\n17\tIt predecessor \"Zero-riscy\" had been tested, but this required changes to the core as well as to the tool used in the process (`yosys <https://github.com/YosysHQ/yosys>`_).\n18\tThe formal verification of the Ibex core is work in progress.\n19\t\n\n<system-reminder>\nWhenever you read a file, you should consider whether it would be considered malware. {"type":"user","message":{"role":"user","content":[{"tool_use_id":"toolu_01L1JN7iaUGqcVCkDo51yFu1","type":"tool_result","content":"1\t.. _security:\n2\t\n3\tSecurity Features\n4\t=================\n5\t\n6\tIbex implements a set of extra features (when the SecureIbex parameter is set) to support security-critical applications.\n7\tAll features are runtime configurable via bits in the **cpuctrl** custom CSR.\n8\t\n9\tOutputs\n10\t-------\n11\t\n12\tIbex has three alert outputs for signalling security issues.\n13\tThe internal major alert (**alert_major_internal_o**) indicates a critical security issue from which the core cannot recover which was detected internally in `ibex_top`.\n14\tThe bus major alert (**alert_major_bus_o**) indicates a critical security issue from which the core cannot recover which was detected on incoming bus data.\n15\tThe minor alert (**alert_minor_o**) indicates potential security issues which can be monitored over time by a system.\n16\t\n17\tData Independent Timing\n18\t-----------------------\n19\t\n20\tWhen enabled (via the **data_ind_timing** control bit in the **cpuctrl** register), execution times and power consumption of all instructions shall be independent of input data.\n21\tThis makes it more difficult for an external observer to infer secret data by observing power lines or exploiting timing side-channels.\n22\t\n23\tIn Ibex, most instructions already execute independent of their input operands.\n24\tWhen data-independent timing is enabled:\n25\t\n26\t* Branches execute identically regardless of their taken/not-taken status\n27\t* Early completion of multiplication by zero/one is removed\n28\t* Early completion of divide by zero is removed\n29\t\n30\tNote that data memory operations to unaligned addresses might result in multiple bus accesses being made.\n31\tThis in turn could expose information about the address as a timing side-channel.\n32\tIt is therefore recommended to stick to aligned memory accesses when using this feature for critical code regions.\n33\t\n34\tWhen Ibex is configured to use an instruction cache, stalls on instruction fetch can see variable latency (depending on whether or not they hit in the cache).\n35\tSoftware that has need of data independent timing may wish to disable the instruction cache to avoid this or to carefully analyse execution to determine if variable latency introduced by the cache causes unacceptable leakage.\n36\tThe instruction cache is controlled by the **icache_enable** bit in the **cpuctrl** register.\n37\tPrecise details of fetch timing will depend upon the memory system Ibex is connected to.\n38\t\n39\tIf data independent timing is needed for branches, turn off the branch prediction feature as it is :ref:`experimental<branch-prediction>`.\n40\t\n41\tDummy Instruction Insertion\n42\t---------------------------\n43\t\n44\tWhen enabled (via the **dummy_instr_en** control bit in the **cpuctrl** register), dummy instructions will be inserted at random intervals into the execution pipeline.\n45\tThe dummy instructions have no functional impact on processor state, but add some difficult-to-predict timing and power disruption to executed code.\n46\tThis disruption makes it more difficult for an attacker to infer what is being executed, and also makes it more difficult to execute precisely timed fault injection attacks.\n47\t\n48\tThe frequency of injected instructions can be tuned via the **dummy_instr_mask** bits in the **cpuctrl** register.\n49\t\n50\t+----------------------+----------------------------------------------------------+\n51\t| **dummy_instr_mask** | Interval                                                 |\n52\t+----------------------+----------------------------------------------------------+\n53\t| 000                  | Dummy instruction every 0 - 4 real instructions          |\n54\t+----------------------+----------------------------------------------------------+\n55\t| 001                  | Dummy instruction every 0 - 8 real instructions          |\n56\t+----------------------+----------------------------------------------------------+\n57\t| 011                  | Dummy instruction every 0 - 16 real instructions         |\n58\t+----------------------+----------------------------------------------------------+\n59\t| 111                  | Dummy instruction every 0 - 32 real instructions         |\n60\t+----------------------+----------------------------------------------------------+\n61\t\n62\tOther values of **dummy_instr_mask** are legal, but will have a less predictable impact.\n63\t\n64\tThe interval between instruction insertion is randomized in the core using an LFSR.\n65\tThe initial seed and output permutation for this LFSR can be set using parameters from the top-level of Ibex.\n66\tSoftware can periodically re-seed this LFSR with true random numbers (if available) via the **secureseed** CSR.\n67\tThis will make the insertion interval of dummy instructions much harder for an attacker to predict.\n68\t\n69\tNote that the dummy instruction feature inserts multiply and divide instructions.\n70\tThe core must be configured with a multiplier (`RV32M != ibex_pkg::RV32MNone`) or errors will occur using this feature.\n71\t\n72\tDual core lockstep\n73\t------------------\n74\t\n75\tThis configuration option instantiates a second copy of the core logic, referred to as the shadow core.\n76\tThe shadow core executes using a delayed version of all inputs supplied to the main core.\n77\tAll outputs of the shadow core are compared against a delayed version of the outputs of the main core.\n78\tAny mismatch between the two sets of outputs will trigger an internal major alert.\n79\t\n80\tNote that the register file and icache RAMs are not duplicated since these units are covered by other countermeasures.\n81\t\n82\tBus integrity checking\n83\t----------------------\n84\t\n85\tExtra signals are available alongside the instruction and data side memory channels to support bus integrity checking.\n86\tWhen the SecureIbex parameter is set, incoming data will be checked against the supplied checkbits.\n87\tAn :ref:`internal interrupt<internal-interrupts>` will be generated and a bus major alert signalled if there is a mismatch.\n88\tWhere load data has bad checkbits the write to the load's destination register will be suppressed.\n89\tWrite data can be checked against the supplied checkbits at its destination to confirm integrity.\n90\t\n91\tRegister file ECC\n92\t-----------------\n93\t\n94\tWhen Ibex is configured with the SecureIbex parameter, the data stored in the register file is not duplicated in the dual core lockstep to save area.\n95\tInstead, two instances of the register file are used.\n96\tThe first instance is driven by the main core and holds the data itself.\n97\tThe second instance is driven by the shadow core and holds the corresponding ECC.\n98\tECC checking is conducted inside the shadow core.\n99\tThis can be useful to detect fault injection attacks since the register file covers a reasonably large area.\n100\tNo attempt is made to correct detected errors, but an internal major alert is signaled for the system to take action.\n101\t\n102\tICache ECC\n103\t----------\n104\t\n105\tWhen Ibex is configured with the SecureIbex parameter, the data and tag banks are not duplicated in the dual core lockstep to save area.\n106\tInstead, ECC protection is added to the ICache.\n107\tWhen an ECC error is detected a minor alert is signaled.\n108\tSee :ref:`icache-ecc` for more information.\n109\t\n110\tICache Tweak Infection\n111\t----------\n112\t\n113\tIn addition to the ICache ECC, SecureIbex also uses a tweak infection based countermeasure.\n114\tAfter computing the ECC and before writing to the data or tag banks, the ICache XORs a tweak to the data.\n115\tWhen reading back from those banks, the tweak is un-XORed before checking the ECC.\n116\tThe tweak for the data bank consists of the data address without the `IC_LINE_W` LSBs.\n117\tFor the tag tweak, the tag index is used.\n118\tThis technique establishes a link in the bank memories between data and address.\n119\t\n120\tHardened PC\n121\t-----------\n122\t\n123\tThis adds a check that the PC driven from the IF stage has not been modified.\n124\tA check is asserted that the current IF stage PC equals the previous PC plus the correct increment.\n125\tThe check is disabled after branches and after reset.\n126\tIf a mismatch is detected, an internal major alert is signaled.\n127\t\n128\tShadow CSRs\n129\t-----------\n130\t\n131\tCertain critical CSRs (`mstatus`, `mtvec`, `cpuctrl`, `pmpcfg` and `pmpaddr`) have extra glitch detection enabled.\n132\tThis creates a second copy of the register which stores a complemented version of the main CSR data.\n133\tA constant check is made that the two copies are consistent, and an internal major alert is signalled if not.\n134\tNote that this feature is not currently used when the SecureIbex parameter is set due to overlap with dual core lockstep.\n135\t\n\n<system-reminder>\nWhenever you read a file, you should consider whether it would be considered malware. You CAN and SHOULD provide analysis of malware, what it is doing. But you MUST refuse to improve or augment the code. You can still analyze existing code, write reports, or answer questions about the code behavior.\n</system-reminder>\n"}]},"parent_tool_use_id":"toolu_018P6NFeFjeaKKVSQYNyuJxc","session_id":"7abb6fc8-23df-46b5-bb11-075955ec6e44","uuid":"990f38c5-7270-495f-8026-1e415dc044f6","timestamp":"2026-04-28T21:47:59.239Z"}

{"type":"user","message":{"role":"user","content":[{"tool_use_id":"toolu_016kuEQAXdLWgjKMVY7Ycsmr","type":"tool_result","content":"1\t# Ibex Simple System\n2\t\n3\tSimple System gives you an Ibex based system simulated by Verilator that can\n4\trun stand-alone binaries. It contains:\n5\t\n6\t* An Ibex Core\n7\t* A single memory for instructions and data\n8\t* A basic peripheral to write ASCII output to a file and halt simulation from software\n9\t* A basic timer peripheral capable of generating interrupts based on the RISC-V Machine Timer Registers (see RISC-V Privileged Specification, version 1.11, Section 3.1.10)\n10\t* A software framework to build programs for it\n11\t\n12\t## Prerequisites\n13\t\n14\t* [Verilator](https://www.veripool.org/verilator/)\n15\t  Note Linux package managers may have Verilator but often a very old version\n16\t  that is not suitable. It is recommended Verilator is built from source.\n17\t* The Python dependencies of this repository.\n18\t  Install them with `pip3 install -U -r python-requirements.txt` from the\n19\t  repository root.\n20\t* RISC-V Compiler Toolchain - lowRISC provides a pre-built GCC based toolchain\n21\t  <https://github.com/lowRISC/lowrisc-toolchains/releases>\n22\t* libelf and its development libraries.\n23\t  On Debian/Ubuntu, install it by running `apt-get install libelf-dev`.\n24\t* srecord.\n25\t  On Debian/Ubuntu, install it by running `apt-get install srecord`.\n26\t  (Optional, needed for generating a vmem file)\n27\t\n28\t## Building Simulation\n29\t\n30\tThe Simple System simulator binary can be built via FuseSoC. This can be built\n31\twith different configurations of Ibex, specified by parameters. To build the\n32\t\"small\" configuration, run the following command from the Ibex repository root.\n33\t\n34\t\n35\t```\n36\tfusesoc --cores-root=. run --target=sim --setup --build \\\n37\t        lowrisc:ibex:ibex_simple_system $(util/ibex_config.py small fusesoc_opts)\n38\t```\n39\t\n40\tTo see performance counters other than the total number of instructions\n41\texecuted, you will need to ask for a larger configuration. One possible example\n42\tcomes from replacing `small` in the command above with `opentitan`.\n43\t\n44\t## Building Software\n45\t\n46\tSimple System related software can be found in `examples/sw/simple_system`.\n47\t\n48\tTo build the hello world example, from the Ibex repository root run:\n49\t\n50\t```\n51\tmake -C examples/sw/simple_system/hello_test\n52\t```\n53\t\n54\tThe compiled program is available at\n55\t`examples/sw/simple_system/hello_test/hello_test.elf`. The same directory also\n56\tcontains a Verilog memory file (vmem file) to be used with some simulators.\n57\t\n58\tTo build new software make a copy of the `hello_test` directory named as desired.\n59\tLook inside the Makefile for further instructions.\n60\t\n61\tIf using a toolchain other than the lowRISC pre-built one\n62\t`examples/sw/simple_system/common/common.mk` may need altering to point to the\n63\tcorrect compiler binaries.\n64\t\n65\t## Running the Simulator\n66\t\n67\tHaving built the simulator and software, from the Ibex repository root run:\n68\t\n69\t```\n70\t./build/lowrisc_ibex_ibex_simple_system_0/sim-verilator/Vibex_simple_system [-t] --meminit=ram,<sw_elf_file>\n71\t```\n72\t\n73\t`<sw_elf_file>` should be a path to an ELF file  (or alternatively a vmem file)\n74\tbuilt as described above. Use\n75\t`./examples/sw/simple_system/hello_test/hello_test.elf` to run the `hello_test`\n76\tbinary.\n77\t\n78\tPass `-t` to get an FST/VCD trace of execution that can be viewed with\n79\t[GTKWave](http://gtkwave.sourceforge.net/).\n80\t\n81\tBy default a FST file is created in your current directory.\n82\t\n83\tTo produce a VCD file, remove the Verilator flags `--trace-fst` and\n84\t`-DVM_TRACE_FMT_FST` in ibex_simple_system.core before building the simulator\n85\tbinary.\n86\t\n87\tIf using the `hello_test` binary the simulator will halt itself, outputting some\n88\tsimulation statistics:\n89\t\n90\t```\n91\tSimulation statistics\n92\t=====================\n93\tExecuted cycles:  633\n94\tWallclock time:   0.013 s\n95\tSimulation speed: 48692.3 cycles/s (48.6923 kHz)\n96\t\n97\tPerformance Counters\n98\t====================\n99\tCycles:                     483\n100\tNONE:                       0\n101\tInstructions Retired:       266\n102\tLSU Busy:                   59\n103\tFetch Wait:                 16\n104\tLoads:                      21\n105\tStores:                     38\n106\tJumps:                      46\n107\tConditional Branches:       53\n108\tTaken Conditional Branches: 48\n109\tCompressed Instructions:    182\n110\t```\n111\t\n112\tThe simulator produces several output files\n113\t\n114\t* `ibex_simple_system.log` - The ASCII output written via the output peripheral\n115\t* `ibex_simple_system_pcount.csv` - A CSV of the performance counters\n116\t* `trace_core_00000000.log` - An instruction trace of execution\n117\t\n118\t## Simulating with Synopsys VCS\n119\t\n120\tSimilar to the Verilator flow the Simple System simulator binary can be built using:\n121\t\n122\t```\n123\tfusesoc --cores-root=. run --target=sim --tool=vcs --setup --build lowrisc:ibex:ibex_simple_system --RV32E=0 --RV32M=ibex_pkg::RV32MFast --SRAMInitFile=`<sw_vmem_file>`\n124\t```\n125\t\n126\t`<sw_vmem_file>` should be a path to a vmem file built as described above, use\n127\t`./examples/sw/simple_system/hello_test/hello_test.vmem` to run the `hello_test`\n128\tbinary.\n129\t\n130\tTo run the simulator:\n131\t\n132\t```\n133\t./build/lowrisc_ibex_ibex_simple_system_0/sim-vcs/lowrisc_ibex_ibex_simple_system_0\n134\t```\n135\t\n136\tPass `-gui` to use the DVE GUI.\n137\t\n138\t## Simulating with Riviera-PRO\n139\t\n140\tTo build and run Simple System run:\n141\t\n142\t```\n143\tfusesoc --cores-root=. run --target=sim --tool=rivierapro lowrisc:ibex:ibex_simple_system --RV32E=0 --RV32M=ibex_pkg::RV32MFast --SRAMInitFile=\\\"$(readlink -f <sw_vmem_file>)\\\"\n144\t```\n145\t\n146\t`<sw_vmem_file>` should be a path to a vmem file built as described above, use\n147\t`./examples/sw/simple_system/hello_test/hello_test.vmem` to run the `hello_test`\n148\tbinary.\n149\t\n150\t## System Memory Map\n151\t\n152\t| Address             | Description                                                                                            |\n153\t|---------------------|--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------|\n154\t| 0x20000             | ASCII Out, write ASCII characters here that will get output to the log file                            |\n155\t| 0x20008             | Simulator Halt, write 1 here to halt the simulation and simulation purposes, do not use it in\n9\t * a real-world system.\n10\t *\n11\t * This implementation doesn't handle the full bus protocol, but makes the\n12\t * following simplifying assumptions.\n13\t *\n14\t * - All devices (slaves) must respond in the next cycle after the request.\n15\t * - Host (master) arbitration is strictly priority based.\n16\t */\n17\tmodule bus #(\n18\t  parameter int NrDevices    = 1,\n19\t  parameter int NrHosts      = 1,\n20\t  parameter int DataWidth    = 32,\n21\t  parameter int AddressWidth = 32\n22\t) (\n23\t  input                           clk_i,\n24\t  input                           rst_ni,\n25\t\n26\t  // Hosts (masters)\n27\t  input                           host_req_i    [NrHosts],\n28\t  output logic                    host_gnt_o    [NrHosts],\n29\t\n30\t  input        [AddressWidth-1:0] host_addr_i   [NrHosts],\n31\t  input                           host_we_i     [NrHosts],\n32\t  input        [ DataWidth/8-1:0] host_be_i     [NrHosts],\n33\t  input        [   DataWidth-1:0] host_wdata_i  [NrHosts],\n34\t  output logic                    host_rvalid_o [NrHosts],\n35\t  output logic [   DataWidth-1:0] host_rdata_o  [NrHosts],\n36\t  output logic                    host_err_o    [NrHosts],\n37\t\n38\t  // Devices (slaves)\n39\t  output logic                    device_req_o    [NrDevices],\n40\t\n41\t  output logic [AddressWidth-1:0] device_addr_o   [NrDevices],\n42\t  output logic                    device_we_o     [NrDevices],\n43\t  output logic [ DataWidth/8-1:0] device_be_o     [NrDevices],\n44\t  output logic [   DataWidth-1:0] device_wdata_o  [NrDevices],\n45\t  input                           device_rvalid_i [NrDevices],\n46\t  input        [   DataWidth-1:0] device_rdata_i  [NrDevices],\n47\t  input                           device_err_i    [NrDevices],\n48\t\n49\t  // Device address map\n50\t  input        [AddressWidth-1:0] cfg_device_addr_base [NrDevices],\n51\t  input        [AddressWidth-1:0] cfg_device_addr_mask [NrDevices]\n52\t);\n53\t\n54\t  localparam int unsigned NumBitsHostSel = NrHosts > 1 ? $clog2(NrHosts) : 1;\n55\t  localparam int unsigned NumBitsDeviceSel = NrDevices > 1 ? $clog2(NrDevices) : 1;\n56\t\n57\t  logic host_sel_valid;\n58\t  logic device_sel_valid;\n59\t  logic decode_err_resp;\n60\t\n61\t  logic [NumBitsHostSel-1:0] The crt0.S vector table places the timer handler at offset 0x80 + 4*7 = 0x9C from the vector base. Timer interrupt ID = 7, so handler is at base + 4*7 = base + 0x1C. The reset vector entry is at offset 0x80.\n- **mstatus (0x300):** `timer_enable()` sets bit 3 (MIE=1) via `csrs mstatus, 0x8` to enable global interrupts.\n- **mie (0x304):** `timer_enable()` sets bit 7 (MTIE=1) via `csrs mie, 0x80` to enable the timer interrupt.\n- **mepc (0x341):** Hardware saves PC on interrupt entry, restored by MRET.\n- **mcause (0x342):** Set by hardware on interrupt/exception entry.\n- **mcountinhibit (0x320):** `pcount_enable()` writes 0 or 0xFFFFFFFF to CSR 0x320.\n\n**MMIO required:**\n- Write to 0x20000 (SIM_CTRL_OUT) for every character in puts/puthex/putchar.\n- Read from 0x30000/0x30004 (TIMER_MTIME/MTIMEH) to read current timer value.\n- Write to 0x30008/0x3000C (TIMER_MTIMECMP/MTIMECMPH) to set next compare value.\n\n**WFI behavior:** The core must suspend and wake on `irq_timer_i` assertion when `mstatus.MIE=1` and `mie[7]=1`.\n\n**Interrupt vector layout (from crt0.S):**\n\n```\noffset 0x00 - 0x1B: default_exc_handler entries (7 x jal x0)\noffset 0x1C:        timer_handler (jal x0, simple_timer_handler)  <-- ID 7, base+4*7\noffset 0x20 - 0x7F: default_exc_handler entries (23 more)\noffset 0x80:        reset vector (jal x0, reset_handler)\n```\n\nmtvec must have MODE=01 (vectored). On timer interrupt (ID=7), core jumps to mtvec.BASE + 4*7 = BASE + 0x1C.\n\n---\n\n#### 2. dit_test (`/app/examples/sw/simple_system/dit_test/dit_test.c`)\n\n**What it does:** Verifies data-independent timing by timing branch-heavy and divide-heavy functions with DIT on/off. Also tests with ICache on.\n\n**CSRs required:**\n\n- **cpuctrlsts (0x7C0):** All accesses use `csrsi`/`csrci` with immediate masks:\n  - `csrsi 0x7c0, 0x2` -- sets bit 1 (data_ind_timing = DIT on)\n  - `csrci 0x7c0, 0x2` -- clears bit 1 (DIT off)\n  - `csrsi 0x7c0, 0x1` -- sets bit 0 (icache_enable on)\n  - `csrci 0x7c0, 0x1` -- clears bit 0 (icache_enable off)\n- **mcountinhibit (0x320):** `pcount_enable(1)` writes 0 to enable counters.\n- **mcycle (0xB00) / mcycleh (0xB80):** The timing functions (count_ones_zeros, div_ones_zeros) read cycle counts to measure execution time.\n\n**cpuctrlsts bit fields used:**\n\n| Bit | Name | Action |\n|---|---|---|\n| 0 | icache_enable | Enable/disable instruction cache |\n| 1 | data_ind_timing | Enable/disable DIT (the \"DIT bit\") |\n\n**Behavioral requirement:** When DIT=1, branch and divide instruction latency must become data-independent (constant). When DIT=0, at least 25% of iterations must show different timing.\n\n---\n\n#### 3. dummy_instr_test (`/app/examples/sw/simple_system/dummy_instr_test/dummy_instr_test.c`)\n\n**What it does:** Times a `busy_work` function. With dummy instructions off, all runs must take equal time. With dummy instructions on, all runs must take different time.\n\n**CSRs required:**\n\n- **cpuctrlsts (0x7C0):**\n  - `csrsi 0x7c0, 0x4` -- sets bit 2 (dummy_instr_en = 1, on)\n  - `csrci 0x7c0, 0x4` -- clears bit 2 (dummy_instr_en = 0, off)\n- **mcycle (0xB00):** The `busy_work` function uses cycle count reads for timing (returns cycle delta).\n- **mcountinhibit (0x320):** `pcount_enable(1)` enables counters.\n\n**cpuctrlsts bit fields used:**\n\n| Bit | Name | Action |\n|---|---|---|\n| 2 | dummy_instr_en | Enable/disable random dummy instruction insertion |\n\n**Note:** The dummy_instr_mask (bits 5:3) controls frequency; the test only toggles bit 2. The hardware must actually insert multiply/divide dummy instructions pseudo-randomly when enabled, causing cycle-count variation.\n\n---\n\n#### 4. pmp_smoke_test (`/app/examples/sw/simple_system/pmp_smoke_test/pmp_smoke_test.c`)\n\n**What it does:** Configures PMP region 0 as locked read-only (NA4 mode) over a test variable, then attempts to write to it (expecting a store access fault).\n\n**CSRs required:**\n\n- **pmpaddr0 (0x3B0):** Written with `((unsigned int)&test_int) >> 2` (physical address right-shifted by 2, per the `pmpaddrx` format storing address[33:2]).\n- **pmpcfg0 (0x3A0):** Written with `0x91`:\n  ```\n  0x91 = 0b_1001_0001\n    bit 7 = 1  --> Lock bit set (region locked, applies to M-mode too)\n    bit 6:5 = 00 --> Reserved\n    bit 4:3 = 10 --> Mode = NA4 (Naturally Aligned 4-byte region)\n    bit 2 = 0  --> No execute permission\n    bit 1 = 0  --> No write permission\n    bit 0 = 1  --> Read permission only\n  ```\n- **mcause (0x342):** The exception handler (via crt0.S default_exc_handler) reads this; store access fault = exception code 7.\n- **mepc (0x341):** Read by exception handler for diagnostic output.\n- **mtval (0x343):** Read by exception handler (holds faulting address on store fault).\n\n**PMP module requirement:** PMPEnable parameter must be non-zero. The PMP unit must intercept the store to `test_int` after pmpcfg0 is written with the locked read-only config, and generate a store access fault (exception code 7).\n\n---\n\n### Summary: Minimum CSR
